{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"{\\\"id\\\": \\\"12\\\", \\\"title\\\": \\\"Anarchism\\\", \\\"text\\\": \\\"Anarchism is a political philosophy and movement that is skeptical of all justifications for authority and seeks to abolish the institutions it claims maintain unnecessary coercion and hierarchy, typically including nation-states, and capitalism. Anarchism advocates for the replacement of the state with stateless societies and voluntary free associations. As a historically left-wing movement, this reading of anarchism is placed on the farthest left of the political spectrum, usually described as the libertarian wing of the socialist movement (libertarian socialism).\\\\n\\\\nHumans have lived in societies without formal hierarchies long before the establishment of states, realms, or empires. With the rise of organised hierarchical bodies, scepticism toward authority also rose. Although traces of anarchist ideas are found all throughout history, modern anarchism emerged from the Enlightenment. During the latter half of the 19th and the first decades of the 20th century, the anarchist movement flourished in most parts of the world and had a significant role in workers' struggles for emancipation. Various anarchist schools of thought formed during this period. Anarchists have taken part in several revolutions, most notably in the Paris Commune, the Russian Civil War and the Spanish Civil War, whose end marked the end of the classical era of anarchism. In the last decades of the 20th and into the 21st century, the anarchist movement has been resurgent once more, growing in popularity and influence within anti-capitalist, anti-war and anti-globalisation movements.\\\\n\\\\nAnarchists employ diverse approaches, which may be generally divided into revolutionary and evolutionary strategies; there is significant overlap between the two. Evolutionary methods try to simulate what an anarchist society might be like, but revolutionary tactics, which have historically taken a violent turn, aim to overthrow authority and the state. Many facets of human civilization have been influenced by anarchist theory, critique, and praxis.\\\\n\\\\nEtymology, terminology, and definition \\\\n\\\\nThe etymological origin of anarchism is from the Ancient Greek anarkhia, meaning \\\\\\\"without a ruler\\\\\\\", composed of the prefix an- (\\\\\\\"without\\\\\\\") and the word arkhos (\\\\\\\"leader\\\\\\\" or \\\\\\\"ruler\\\\\\\"). The suffix -ism denotes the ideological current that favours anarchy. Anarchism appears in English from 1642 as anarchisme and anarchy from 1539; early English usages emphasised a sense of disorder. So in LOGO, when you hold out one generator (e.g., qwen3-8b), the gen-0 samples in the training set (from other generators) will have THE EXACT SAME TEXT as the gen-0 samples in the test set (from qwen3-8b). 

This is direct data leakage that confirms C2—the 99.4% gen-0 accuracy is almost certainly inflated by this contamination. Now I'm looking at the per-generator F1 scores from Experiment A to compute the statistics for Q1.

Computing the mean and standard deviation across the six models, I get an average F1 of about 0.789 with individual deviations ranging from -0.107 to +0.064, which I'm now squaring to calculate the variance. Now I'm calculating the deviations from that mean of 0.7964 and squaring them to get the variance—the standard deviation comes out to about 0.0416.

Looking at the range, the difference between the highest and lowest values is 0.1239, or about 12.4 percentage points. Interestingly, the 4-baseline ensemble shows lower per-generator standard deviation (0.042) and a tighter range (12.4pp) compared to the 7-feature ensemble (0.055 std, 17.1pp range), which is actually the opposite of what the paper might expect. This is a key finding for evaluating whether the baseline approach maintains consistency across generators.

Now I need to calculate the standard deviation for the 4-baseline+surface variant. I'm computing the mean F1 score across the six generators and then finding the deviations from that mean to determine how much variation exists in this configuration. I'm realizing the 4-baseline ensembles actually show better consistency across generators than the 7-feature ensemble, which is surprising. But I need to reconsider what I'm comparing — the paper's Table 3 contrasts the 7-feature ensemble against individual detectors, not baseline ensembles, and those individual detector confidence intervals are bootstrap estimates of per-fold F1, not per-generator performance. Let me look at the actual per-generator F1 scores from the individual detectors in plan-015-v2 to make a proper comparison. Looking at the per-generator F1 scores across the LOGO folds, I see that the tight confidence interval for the 7-feature ensemble in the 5-gen case is somewhat misleading — it's partly because qwen3-8b, which is a significant outlier, wasn't included in that fold. When I include it in the 6-gen analysis, the per-generator range jumps to 17.1 percentage points, which is actually quite substantial. So while per-generator standard deviation is a valid consistency metric, the 7-feature ensemble's performance isn't as consistent as that narrow CI suggests.

Comparing across ensembles, the 4-baseline ensemble actually shows lower per-generator variability at 0.042 with a 12.4pp range, and individual detectors would likely have even higher variance based on the wide confidence intervals I'm seeing. So the substitution would technically work but would expose that the 7-feature ensemble isn't as consistent as claimed — still better than individual detectors, but worse than the baseline ensemble.

For the seed sharing question, I've confirmed it: both olmo-2-1b and qwen3-8b use seed_id=0 at depth=0 with the exact same Wikipedia article (Anarchism), drawn from a shared pool of 1000 Wikipedia articles used across all generators.

On the instruction-tuned models versus scope reduction within 10 days, both are realistic for an AI team — instruction-tuned experiments would take about 2 days of GPU time plus code adaptation to generate chains, extract features, and run LOGO, while a scope rewrite is just writing and takes less than a day. The smart move is doing both since the scope rewrite is cheap insurance and adding 2-3 instruction models directly addresses the concern, though the real question is whether the depth response pattern holds for instruction-tuned models, and if it doesn't, the paper's core contribution becomes much weaker.

For the ordinal regression experiment, with only 3 classes the gains are likely minimal since ordinal methods like CORAL really benefit from many ordered classes, making multiclass XGBoost probably equivalent. It's worth running anyway since it's low-cost and even a null result directly answers the reviewer's concern.

On extracting a single baseline at 200/6 from Experiment A, that's not possible because Experiment A only ran ensemble comparisons.  The individual detector scores there are per-feature-in-ensemble, not standalone. I'd need to train separate single-feature XGBoost models with max_depth=6 using the same LOGO splits — though actually, I should check if plan-015-v2 already has individual baselines. It does, but those used different hyperparams (100/3), so to properly answer M6 I need to run the individual baselines again with 200/6 to show the direct comparison.

Now I'm going through the reviewer's questions: Wikipedia seeds are confirmed shared, instruction-tuning was tested, per-generation F1 standard deviations are addressed, and the 200/6 baselines need that new run. The paper already discusses why the 7-feature approach was chosen over multi-detector ensembles in section 4.3, though the framing could be clearer. But I'm noticing several items that haven't been addressed yet — the Pythia-1B family bias cross-reference, the shape features versus fixed temperature question, and the Qwen3-8B volume control improvement. These gaps need to be filled in the action matrix.

{
  "raw": "结果已保存到 `results/review_p2f_cross_ref_bias.json`。\n\n**核心发现：Same-family bias 不成立。**\n\n| Reference Model | Pythia-1B F1 | 其他4gen平均 | Pythia-1B偏差 | Pythia-1B排名 |\n|----------------|-------------|-------------|--------------|--------------|\n| Pythia-410M (同族) | 0.856 | 0.790 | +0.065 | **#1** |\n| OLMo-2-1B (跨族) | 0.845 | 0.798 | +0.048 | **#1** |\n| Qwen3-0.6B (跨族) | 0.848 | 0.808 | +0.041 | **#1** |\n\n- Pythia-1B 在**所有三种** reference model 下都排名第一\n- 同族→跨族 F1 下降仅 0.009（0.856→0.847），远在 CI 范围内\n- Pythia-1B 的高可检测性是其生成模式的内在特征，不是 reference model 家族偏差"
}
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The results are clear. Let me verify:

1. **Pythia-1B F1 across reference models:**
   - Pythia-410M (same family): 0.8555
   - OLMo-2-1B (cross family): 0.8454
   - Qwen3-0.6B (cross family): 0.8483
   - Cross-family average: (0.8454 + 0.8483) / 2 = 0.8468
   - Drop from same to cross: 0.8555 - 0.8468 = 0.0087 (~0.9pp)
   - Pythia-1B ranks first across all three reference models.

Now I'm noticing this is actually a 5-generator LOGO setup with olmo-2-1b, pythia-1b, pythia-2.8b, qwen3-0.6b, and qwen3-1.7b — qwen3-8b isn't included in the per-generator breakdown. Looking at the worker results table, it mentions "其他4gen平均" which appears to be averaging across the other four generators.

There's an interesting overlap I'm catching: when qwen3-0.6b serves as the reference model for feature extraction, it's also one of the generators being evaluated. This creates a potential same-family bias where qwen3-0.6b-generated text gets evaluated using features computed from the same model family, rather than having a truly independent reference model.

But circling back to the original question about Pythia-1B—the data makes it clear that same-family bias isn't an issue there. I'm also noticing the worker's reported numbers have minor rounding differences from the actual values (0.856 vs 0.8555, 0.845 vs 0.8454), which checks out. And comparing across experiments, Pythia-1B's F1 score in Exp A (0.8528 with 7 features and 6 generators) aligns reasonably well with the cross-reference analysis showing 0.8555 under the Pythia-410M reference model with 5 generators.

The performance drop when using cross-family references is minimal—only about 0.9 percentage points—and Pythia-1B still maintains its top ranking. What's interesting is that the gap between Pythia-1B and the average of other models actually narrows with cross-family references (0.048 and 0.041 versus 0.065 with same-family), suggesting the same-family setup does amplify Pythia-1B's advantage somewhat, but doesn't fundamentally create it.

Now I should verify whether this analysis used the existing cross-reference model data files.
```

_source line 303 · 2026-05-15T13:49:12.099Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a complete picture. Let me analyze:

1. **Model identity issue**: The existing `qwen3-1.7b` in the base experiments uses `model: "qwen3-1.7b"` (raw continuation, no chat template). The new instruct experiment uses the same model but with `apply_chat_template`. Looking at the run script: `MODEL_PATH="/root/autodl-tmp/models/Qwen/Qwen3-1___7B"`.

This is potentially the same model used in both base and instruct experiments (Qwen3-1.7B). The difference is just the prompting method (raw continuation vs chat template). 

So this is really testing whether the chat template interface changes the depth signal when applied to the same underlying model. But I need to verify whether Qwen3 actually has separate base and instruct versions — if all Qwen3 models are instruction-tuned by default, then the "base" experiments are already using instruction-tuned weights in raw continuation mode, and adding the chat template would just be a different interface on top of the same model.

Looking at the model paths, `Qwen3-1___7B` appears to be the base model (with underscores replacing dots in the filename), while the instruct version would be something like `Qwen3-1___7B-Instruct`. The script applies a chat template to whatever model it loads, but if the base model doesn't actually have a chat template defined, this could fail or produce unexpected output — though Qwen3 base models might still have the template in their tokenizer config even if they're not instruction-tuned.

The existing generations data for qwen3-1.7b shows severe repetition and hallucination patterns that look characteristic of a base model rather than an instruct model, which would typically be more coherent. The analysis script compares Spearman correlation values against hardcoded thresholds for the base model variant, suggesting the experiment might actually be testing a base model with structured prompting rather than a true instruct model as the reviewer requested.

The methodology itself is sound, though the correlation thresholds of 0.3 and 0.15 are somewhat arbitrary, and testing only one instruct model provides a preliminary check but isn't enough for a strong claim without LOGO evaluation for direct F1 comparison. There's also the persistent issue of gen-0 seed leakage if this model gets added to the broader evaluation set.

I'm noticing a critical concern about the model identity—the path `Qwen3-1___7B` likely points to the base Qwen3-1.7B rather than the instruction-tuned variant, which means it might not fully satisfy the requirement for RLHF-trained models. The sampling parameters and correlation analysis are consistent with the earlier approach, but I can't verify the actual results yet since the experiment is still running. Let me double-check the Qwen3 model lineup to clarify which version is actually being used.

Looking at the April 2025 Qwen3 release, it appears Alibaba unified their base and instruction-tuned variants into a single model with thinking capabilities, so there may not be a separate base version at all. If that's the case, then using Qwen3 models as "base" models for raw continuation is already problematic—the paper should acknowledge that these are inherently instruction-tuned, which contradicts their classification.

The instruct experiment then becomes a test of the same model with and without chat templates, which is useful for showing that formatting preserves depth signals, but less compelling than comparing genuinely distinct base and instruct variants like Llama-3 or Mistral. For Pythia and OLMo, the concern is more valid since those are true base models.

I should document these observations in my review and flag the model identity issue, especially since the experiment is still running.
```

{
  "text": "## 预审报告 — review-p1c-instruct（方法论审查，实验仍在运行）\n\n实验在 autodl-5090 cuda:1 运行中（PID 211205, 4050 MiB），预计 ~80 min 完成。以下先审方法论设计。\n\n### 发现\n\n- [severity: warning] **模型身份需确认**。run 脚本中 `MODEL_PATH=\"/root/autodl-tmp/models/Qwen/Qwen3-1___7B\"`（即 Qwen3-1.7B），**不含 \"Instruct\" 后缀**。Qwen3 系列所有模型（2025年4月发布）出厂即带指令微调+思考模式，没有独立的 base 版本——因此 Qwen3-1.7B 本身就是 instruction-tuned model。\n\n  这带来两个问题：\n  1. **论文原有的 Qwen3 \"base model\" 定位存疑**：原实验中 qwen3-0.6b/1.7b/8b 也是同一 instruction-tuned 模型，只是用 raw continuation 模式（无 chat template）。论文称 \"全部为 base/pretrained 模型\" 对 Qwen3 系列不准确。\n  2. **本实验的 \"instruct vs base\" 对比实际上是 \"同一模型 + chat template vs 同一模型 + raw continuation\"**，不是真正的 base vs instruct 对比。这仍有价值（测试 chat interface 是否改变 depth signal），但不能完全回答 C3（审稿人要的是 instruction-tuned/RLHF 模型的 depth response）。\n\n  **建议**：(1) 论文中对 Qwen3 模型加注释：\"Qwen3 models are instruction-tuned by default; we use them in raw continuation mode without chat template\"；(2) 考虑追加一个有明确 base/instruct 分离的模型（如 OLMo-2-1B-Instruct，若存在）以更强地回答 C3。\n\n- [severity: info] **采样参数一致**。Instruct 脚本使用 temperature=0.7, top_p=0.9, max_new_tokens=256——与 base model 实验完全一致。公平对比。\n\n- [severity: info] **chat template 设计合理**。System prompt: \"Continue the following text naturally. Output only the continuation, without repeating the original text or adding any preamble.\" + `enable_thinking=False`。保持了 continuation 任务一致性，仅改变了 interface mode。\n\n- [severity: info] **分析脚本方法学合理**。`analyze_instruct.py` 计算 Spearman ρ (feature vs depth)，与 base model 硬编码 ρ 对比，用阈值判断 differential depth response 是否成立（drifting: |ρ|>0.3，shape: |ρ|<0.15）。阈值是合理经验值。\n\n- [severity: info] **局限性**。仅 1 个 instruct 模型 → 不能做 LOGO，只能做 Spearman ρ 模式对比。如果需要 instruct 模型加入 LOGO，需再加 2+ 个 instruct 模型。\n\n- [severity: info] **GPU 合规**。cuda:1，项目无 gpu_ids 约束。进程占用 4050 MiB / 32607 MiB (12.4%)，合理（~1.7B fp16 模型）。\n\n**实验完成后需再次审查**：核实 Spearman ρ 数值、differential depth response 判定结果、与 base model 的对比幅度。",

```text
Now I have both the P0-A (gen-0 leakage) and P1-D (Table 3 6-gen baselines) results. Let me review them together.

**P0-A (gen-0 leakage / binary LOGO)**:
- gen-1 vs gen-2+ binary LOGO (gen-0 excluded): 7-feature F1=0.7233, 4-baseline+surface F1=0.7239
- Both are essentially tied (within CI)
- 7-feature still beats individual detectors


- Binoculars performs near-random on this binary depth task (0.524)

Comparing to the 3-class results, the 3-class 7-feature achieved 0.7887 while binary only got 0.7233 — which seems odd since binary should be easier. But the 3-class included gen-0 which had near-perfect accuracy due to leakage, artificially inflating the macro-F1. The actual depth discrimination between gen-1 and gen-2+ is really just 0.723.

Now looking at the 6-gen baseline results, the hyperparameter sweep confirms the original choices were sound — varying between 100/3 and 200/6 hyperparameters made negligible difference. But there's a concerning drop in AvgLogProb when moving from 5-gen to 6-gen data: 0.765 down to 0.737.

The bigger issue is the inconsistency across detectors — LD-Score plummeted 6.6 percentage points while FastDetectGPT actually improved by 6.1 points, and Binoculars collapsed by 16.2 points. The worker's note about the pipeline potentially being different makes sense here. Adding a weaker generator like qwen3-8b would explain the AvgLogProb and LD-Score declines, but it doesn't account for FastDetectGPT's improvement or Binoculars' dramatic collapse, which suggests something more fundamental changed in how the data was processed.

The most plausible explanation is that plan-015-v2 employed a different evaluation setup entirely — different classifier, different downsampling strategy, or different LOGO implementation. This means the old and new Table 3 results aren't directly comparable, so while replacing the numbers with the new consistent protocol is the right call, the paper's narrative around those original findings needs to be reconsidered. I'm also noticing the AvgLogProb shift from 0.765 to 0.737 at 100/3 tokens is substantial enough to warrant updating Table 2 as well. Let me consolidate these findings across both issues and document them.

Now I'm cross-checking the binary results against the 3-class setup to quantify how much gen-0 was inflating performance. The 3-class model with gen-0 included hits 0.7887 F1, but drops to 0.7233 when gen-0 is excluded in the binary scenario — that's a 6.5 percentage point gap that reflects both the leakage effect and the inherent difficulty change from removing a class. The key takeaway for the paper is that the binary gen-1 vs gen-2+ result of 0.723 should be the reported number going forward.

What's interesting is that in the binary task, my 7-feature ensemble and the 4-baseline+surface ensemble are statistically equivalent — their confidence intervals overlap substantially. This suggests the 3-class advantage I saw for the baseline ensemble was really just gen-0 classification boosting those numbers, not a genuine superiority on the actual depth discrimination task. 7-feature at 0.7233 edges out 4-baseline at 0.7118 by 1.2 percentage points in the binary setting, essentially tying with 4-baseline+surface at 0.7239 — a complete reversal from the 3-class results where 4-baseline dominated. The gen-0 leak was masking 7-feature's actual competitive strength.

This reframes the paper's narrative significantly: the honest binary comparison shows 7-feature holding its own against baseline ensembles, even though the overall F1 drops from ~0.79 to ~0.72 when removing the artificial gen-0 boost. Now I need to cross-check whether the P1-D baseline data aligns with these P0-A findings.

Looking at the individual baseline performance across both datasets, the 3-class task inflates some metrics like AvgLogProb and FDG while deflating Binoculars, which tracks with the inherent difficulty differences between binary and three-way classification. The patterns are internally consistent, so I can now synthesize a comprehensive review of both papers.

|\n|------|-----------|-------------|------------------------|\n| 7-feature | **0.7233** | 0.051 | 0.7887 |\n| 4-baseline+surface | **0.7239** | 0.049 | 0.8049 |\n| 4-baseline | 0.7118 | 0.044 | 7-feature 持平。这说明 Exp A 中 baseline ensemble 的优势**完全来自 gen-0 分类**——baseline detectors 更擅长区分 human-vs-machine（正好是它们的设计目标），但在纯深度任务（gen-1 vs gen-2+）上，7-feature ensemble 恢复了竞争力。\n\n  **这对论文叙事是好消息**：在诚实的评估下（排除泄露），7-feature ensemble 不再落后于 baseline ensemble。论文可以同时报告 3-class（附 gen-0 共享 caveat）和 binary（作为 primary metric），用 binary 结果支撑 contribution。\n\n- [severity: warning] **论文核心 F1 从 <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012w36Dis7YcxbeDBSAfHG2w`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
8	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
9	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
10	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
11	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
12	
13	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
14	\label{sec:generation}
15	
16	We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, and \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}.
17	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
18	
19	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
20	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
21	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
22	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
23	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
24	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
25	The resulting dataset contains approximately 27{,}000 texts across all generators and depths.
26	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
27	
28	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
29	\label{sec:features}
30	
31	The seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.
32	Reference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.
33	Together, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).
34	This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
35	
36	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
37	
38	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
39	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than and architecturally independent of all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
40	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
41	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
42	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
43	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
44	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
45	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
46	    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
47	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
48	    The 90th percentile threshold follows standard practice in heavy-tail analysis \cite{ood_baseline}; sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:tail-threshold}) confirms stable classification across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.
49	\end{itemize}
50	
51	\paragraph{Surface features.}
52	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
53	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
54	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
55	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena}).
56	\end{itemize}
57	
58	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
59	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
60	This differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form---motivates combining both feature types.
61	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
62	
63	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
64	\label{sec:evaluation}
65	
66	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
67	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.
68	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
69	
70	\paragraph{Classifier.}
71	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
72	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
73	We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.
74	Baseline detectors (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\_\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\max\_\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.
75	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
76	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
77	
78	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
79	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
80	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals ($n{=}1{,}000$ resamples).
81	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
82	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
83	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
84	We report six pairwise comparisons in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
85	Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
86	
87	\paragraph{Baselines.}
88	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
89	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
90	
91	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
92	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
93	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.
94	
```

comparisons against detection baselines, we use \\textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.\nAll reported $p$-values are two-sided.\nWe report six pairwise comparisons in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.\nWe conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.\n",

1	% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As LLM outputs accumulate on the web, training corpora increasingly contain recursively generated text of unknown provenance depth.
4	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text as human-written (gen-0), first-generation synthetic (gen-1), or deeper (gen-2+)---beyond binary detection.
5	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features remain near-stationary.
6	A 7-feature ensemble exploiting this complementarity achieves leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 of 0.79 ($p < 0.001$ vs.\ all baselines) across 6 generators from 3 model families (0.6B--8.2B), with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
7	In our setting, depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling, positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization rather than a curative tool for data selection.
8	\end{abstract}
9

### Tool result

_source line 345 · 2026-05-15T13:55:53.425Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SeXR4VBAicvKu6uABxbe9j`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled corpora are the dominant source of pretraining data for large language models (LLMs), yet an increasing fraction of this data is itself LLM-generated.
5	Estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}, and this proportion continues to grow as generated text enters the web and becomes seed material for subsequent generation.
6	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text---a problem that current detection methods are ill-equipped to address.
7	
8	Existing detectors \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt} provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, forcing an all-or-nothing filtering decision: discard everything flagged as synthetic, losing potentially useful first-generation content, or retain everything, admitting text with compounding distributional drift.
9	This conflates qualitatively different material.
10	A first-generation continuation---an LLM expanding a human-written passage---retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties.
11	A third- or fourth-generation text, conditioned recursively on prior LLM outputs, exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity, compressed perplexity, and narrower token probability distributions.
12	Treating these as a single ``synthetic'' category discards provenance information that could inform risk-stratified curation decisions.
13	
14	We argue that a finer-grained characterization is needed: \emph{ordinal depth estimation}, the task of classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label.
15	We refer to the overall framework---features, classifier, and evaluation protocol---as \emph{depth fingerprinting}, distinguishing the method from the task it addresses.
16	No existing method addresses this task directly in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.
17	When repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance---confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1 (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines})---making any single detector unreliable across unknown generators.
18	Concurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence behavior under semantic-preservation constraints, which bound distinguishable depth classes to approximately three.
19	These results do not extend to \emph{non-paraphrastic} generation chains (e.g., continuation), where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.
20	We focus on non-paraphrastic continuation chains from base (pretrained) models; instruction-tuned and RLHF models, which dominate web text, remain untested (Limitations).
21	
22	We study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic LLM generation chains across six generators from three model families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen; 0.6B--8.2B parameters).
23	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy (functionally an NLL-based out-of-distribution score \cite{ood_baseline}), type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
24	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution: text becomes progressively more predictable on average while the functional form of the distribution is preserved.
25	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
26	The practical advantage is reliability across unknown generators.
27	
28	We also report a negative finding.
29	Depth-aware data curation---selecting training data based on estimated generation depth---does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 random vs.\ 2.387 depth-stratified).
30	In our experimental setting, depth fingerprinting is an effective \emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \emph{curative} tool for data selection.
31	
32	Our contributions are:
33	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
34	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for finer-grained provenance characterization of web-crawled text.
35	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We characterize differential depth response across six generators spanning three model families (0.6B--8.2B): a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.79, outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p < 0.001$), with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
36	    \item \textbf{Complementary analysis.} As a complementary analysis, we show that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling, establishing depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic rather than curative tool.
37	\end{enumerate}
38	
39	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
40	\S\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.
41	\S\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.
42	\S\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, baseline comparisons, and the downstream utility experiment.
43	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications, and a separate Limitations section addresses scope boundaries.
44	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
5	
6	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
7	\label{sec:results-classification}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.
10	
11	The 7-feature ensemble achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.789, significantly outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p<0.001$; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
12	Per-generator held-out F1 ranges from 0.683 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.856 (Pythia-1B).
13	Per-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\,=\,0.94, gen-2+\,=\,0.80, gen-1\,=\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.
14	
15	\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}
16	The six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists at scales beyond the $\leq$2.8B training generators.
17	However, cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B held-out F1\,=\,0.683 (CI [0.668, 0.694]), compared to 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators.
18	Text generated by larger models is more fluent, compressing feature margins and making depth distinctions harder.
19	Notably, adding Qwen3-8B data \emph{improves} classification on the original five generators (+1.1\,pp to 0.811), indicating that cross-scale diversity benefits rather than dilutes the training signal.
20	A volume-matched control confirms this gain is driven by generator diversity, not data quantity: upsampling the 4-generator training set to match the 5-generator count yields only +0.05\,pp, while the remaining +0.78\,pp (94\% of the total improvement) is attributable to the additional generator's distributional diversity.
21	We note that this gap may narrow with scale-matched training data; our current dataset contains $\sim$1{,}000 samples per generator regardless of scale.
22	
23	\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}
24	Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\Delta$\,=\,$-$2.6\,pp vs.\ LOGO).
25	
26	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
27	Text length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\rho| < 0.14$).
28	Length-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
29	
30	
31	\begin{table}[t]
32	\centering
33	\small
34	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
35	\toprule
36	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Held-out F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
37	\midrule
38	Pythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & [0.843, 0.863] \\
39	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & [0.793, 0.813] \\
40	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & [0.754, 0.776] \\
41	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & [0.813, 0.834] \\
42	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.805 & [0.796, 0.817] \\
43	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & [0.668, 0.694] \\
44	\midrule
45	\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & --- \\
46	\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.800 & --- \\
47	\bottomrule
48	\end{tabular}
49	\caption{Leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 with bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals ($n$\,=\,1000) for 3-class depth estimation using the 7-feature ensemble. Each row holds out one generator for testing and trains on the remaining five.}
50	\label{tab:logo-results}
51	\end{table}
52	
53	
54	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
55	\label{sec:results-differential}
56	
57	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
58	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
63	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}
64	\label{fig:feature-drift}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
68	drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$),
69	while shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$).
70	
71	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
72	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.
73	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\_ratio; F1\,=\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\_decay; F1\,=\,0.745; McNemar $p \approx 0$).
74	Permutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).
75	Surface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.
76	
77	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
78	To confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we compute features using three cross-family references: Pythia-410M, OLMo-2-1B, and Qwen3-0.6B.
79	All three achieve LOGO F1 between 0.80 and 0.82, with consistent drift directions across features.
80	The pattern generalizes across reference model architectures.
81	
82	
83	\begin{table}[t]
84	\centering
85	\small
86	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
87	\toprule
88	\textbf{Feature set} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\
89	\midrule
90	Full (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\_ratio (0.269) \\
91	Surface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\_ratio (0.320) \\
92	Reference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\_mean (0.218) \\
93	\bottomrule
94	\end{tabular}
95	\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}
96	\label{tab:ablation}
97	\end{table}
98	
99	Leave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
100	
101	
102	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
103	\label{sec:results-baselines}
104	
105	\begin{figure}[t]
106	\centering
107	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}
108	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}
109	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
110	\end{figure}
111	
112	We repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same ordinal classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation (Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).
113	
114	\begin{table}[t]
115	\centering
116	\small
117	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
118	\toprule
119	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
120	\midrule
121	7-feature ensemble & \textbf{0.80} & [0.786, 0.799] \\
122	Avg-LogProb        & 0.765 & [0.616, 0.820] \\
123	LD-Score            & 0.721 & [0.588, 0.770] \\
124	FastDetectGPT       & 0.627 & [0.511, 0.762] \\
125	Binoculars          & 0.579 & [0.405, 0.664] \\
126	\bottomrule
127	\end{tabular}
128	\caption{Ordinal depth classification: 7-feature ensemble vs.\ single detection baselines (5-gen LOGO). The ensemble outperforms the best single detector (Avg-LogProb) by +3.5\,pp (paired bootstrap $p < 0.001$, $\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]).}
129	\label{tab:baselines}
130	\end{table}
131	The ensemble outperforms every \emph{single} detection baseline in mean F1: +3.5\,pp over Avg-LogProb ($p < 0.001$, $\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]), +7.9\,pp over LD-Score, +17.3\,pp over FastDetectGPT, and +22.1\,pp over Binoculars (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
132	
133	The critical distinction is \emph{reliability}.
134	The ensemble achieves per-generator F1 ranging from 0.683 to 0.856 (std\,=\,0.058).
135	While this range reflects genuine difficulty variation across generators---particularly at larger scales (\S\ref{sec:results-classification})---the ensemble provides reliable performance even for the most challenging generator (Qwen3-8B: 0.683).
136	Each single detector captures partial depth signal---Avg-LogProb tracks mean perplexity shift, LD-Score captures character-level distributional change---but each fails on specific generator families.
137	The 7-feature ensemble aggregates complementary signals, producing stable performance regardless of the held-out generator.
138	The overall 95\% bootstrap CI [0.783, 0.792] reflects sampling precision of the pooled estimate, not cross-generator consistency.
139	
140	\paragraph{Multi-detector ensembles.}
141	To test whether combining multiple detection scores yields stronger depth signal, we train XGBoost on all four baseline scores jointly (4-detector ensemble).
142	The 4-detector ensemble achieves 6-gen LOGO F1\,=\,0.796 [0.792, 0.801], outperforming the 7-feature ensemble (0.789) by +0.7\,pp.
143	Adding our surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) to the 4-detector ensemble further improves performance to 0.805 [0.800, 0.809], the strongest configuration tested.
144	This confirms that our surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.
145	However, the 4-detector ensemble requires running four separate detection pipelines, while the 7-feature approach uses a single lightweight reference model (Pythia-410M) plus two vocabulary statistics.
146	The practical advantage of depth features lies in their interpretability---they reveal the differential depth response pattern (\S\ref{sec:results-differential})---and computational efficiency, not raw classification accuracy.
147	
148	
149	\subsection{Downstream Utility: A Negative Finding}
150	\label{sec:results-downstream}
151	
152	Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?
153	We fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:downstream}).
154	
155	\begin{table}[t]
156	\centering
157	\small
158	\begin{tabular}{lrc}
159	\toprule
160	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{Test PPL} \\
161	\midrule
162	All depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
163	Size-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
164	Depth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
165	Binary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
166	Depth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
167	\bottomrule
168	\end{tabular}
169	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}
170	\label{tab:downstream}
171	\end{table}
172	
173	The results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.
174	Training on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).
175	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).
176	Cross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.
177	
178	Restricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\,=\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.
179	
180	We report this negative finding transparently: depth fingerprinting is an effective \emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \emph{curative} tool for data curation.
181	Knowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.
182	
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All six generators are base (pretrained) models.
5	Web text is predominantly produced by instruction-tuned and RLHF-aligned models, whose generation distributions differ from base model continuations.
6	Validating that differential depth response persists under instruction tuning is a critical direction for future work.
7	
8	Our experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).
9	Depth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale classifier transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\,=\,0.683 vs.\ 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators).
10	Frontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
11	
12	We evaluate three prompt types (continuation, elaboration, rewriting; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}) but all share the same Pythia-2.8B generator.
13	Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms the depth signal generalizes, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.
14	
15	Chains are constructed with a single generator model per step.
16	Real-world web contamination likely involves heterogeneous generators at each depth, which could attenuate or alter the depth signal we observe.
17	We also do not evaluate robustness to human editing or post-processing of generated text, which is common in practice and may disrupt distributional features.
18	
19	The reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) suggest the phenomenon is not reference-specific.
20	All seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.
21	
22	The negative downstream result (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}) is evaluated on causal language modeling only.
23	Other downstream tasks (classification, summarization, instruction following) may respond differently to depth composition.
24	
25	Our analysis uses fixed decoding parameters (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p=0.9$). The relationship between sampling strategy and depth signal magnitude remains an open question.
26	
27	We use 3-class granularity (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\,$\approx$\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.
28	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FCeYH9eXngHiTWHwwuMzCo`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why are shape features stationary?}
5	Distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
6	One \emph{speculative} explanation is that shape is primarily determined by the sampling mechanism (temperature, top-$p$ truncation) and the model's architecture-level output distribution, both of which are fixed across generation steps.
7	Recursive generation shifts \emph{what} the model talks about---steering toward higher-probability vocabulary regions---but does not alter \emph{how} the model distributes probability mass around its predictions.
8	If this account is correct, shape features should remain stationary even at much greater depths and across different prompt types, while level features should continue to drift until reaching a fixed point.
9	We flag this as a hypothesis for future investigation, not a conclusion supported by our current experiments.
10	
11	\paragraph{Relation to OOD detection.}
12	Reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores---the same signal backbone underlying DetectGPT \cite{detectgpt}, GLTR \cite{gltr}, and Binoculars \cite{binoculars}.
13	Our contribution is not these features per se, but the \emph{ordinal} depth formulation and the empirical finding that they exhibit differential depth response under recursive generation.
14	This connection also offers a lens on shape stationarity: in the OOD detection literature, distributional location statistics (mean log-likelihood) shift under domain drift, while shape statistics (variance, tail behavior) are more robust \cite{ood_baseline}.
15	Recursive generation chains produce an analogous effect---progressive drift away from human text that manifests primarily as a location shift in the NLL distribution.
16	
17	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
18	The differential depth response enables provenance characterization of text corpora.
19	Rather than binary keep-or-discard filtering, practitioners can use depth estimates to understand the composition of their data.
20	Whether this understanding translates to improved curation outcomes remains an open question (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
21	Three-class granularity separates human text from moderate-drift first-generation content and from deeper text with compounding distributional shift---the distinction most relevant to provenance analysis.
22	Finer-grained depth distinctions may be achievable for non-paraphrastic chains (unlike paraphrastic chains, which converge within $\sim$3 steps \cite{broken_telephone}), but the three-class scheme already captures the practically relevant boundaries.
23	
24	\paragraph{Information value of the negative finding.}
25	Depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling at equal sample count (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
26	This negative result carries positive information value: it rules out depth-based filtering as a data selection strategy, sparing practitioners from building depth-conditional pipelines that would not yield quality gains.
27	The dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \cite{self_consuming_mad}.
28	
29	\paragraph{Connection to model collapse.}
30	Our results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.
31	Model collapse describes distributional degradation when models are \emph{retrained} on their own outputs across generations.
32	We observe analogous drift---monotonic decrease in vocabulary diversity, compression of perplexity---in \emph{inference chains} without any parameter updates.
33	This suggests that the mechanisms documented in model collapse theory (regression toward high-probability modes, loss of distributional tails) operate at inference time as well, driven by the sampling process rather than gradient updates.
34	The shared phenomenology motivates using depth fingerprinting features as early indicators of data that, if fed back into training, may accelerate model collapse.
35	
36	\paragraph{Topic drift as mediator, not confounder.}
37	Recursive generation narrows topic diversity: cosine similarity between adjacent depths increases monotonically from 0.62 (depth 0$\to$1) to 0.86 (depth 3$\to$4), consistent with regression toward high-probability modes.
38	However, topic narrowing accounts for less than 9\% of feature variance (maximum: entropy\_mean, $\rho = -0.286$, $R^2 = 0.082$).
39	Shape features are near-independent of semantic drift (tail\_decay $|\rho| = 0.068$, $R^2 = 0.005$).
40	Topic drift is best understood as a \emph{mediator}---one mechanism through which depth manifests---rather than a confound that invalidates depth features.
41	The depth signal primarily reflects sampling-induced distributional compression, not topic narrowing per se.
42	
43	\paragraph{Depth features vs.\ multi-detector ensembles.}
44	While multi-detector ensembles achieve higher raw accuracy (0.805 with surface features added), depth features offer a single-model, interpretable alternative that reveals the underlying distributional dynamics of recursive generation.
45	
46	\paragraph{Same-family reference bias.}
47	Pythia-1B achieves the highest held-out F1 (0.856) despite using a same-family reference model (Pythia-410M).
48	Cross-family references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield nearly identical Pythia-1B F1 (0.845--0.848), confirming that this generator's high detectability reflects its intrinsic generation patterns rather than reference-model family bias.
49	
```

_source line 366 · 2026-05-15T13:56:39.670Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is the ordinal regression experiment (M5). Let me verify the results:

1. **3-class**: XGBoost 0.7888, CORAL 0.7711, Frank-Hall 0.7931
   - Frank-Hall is +0.43pp over XGBoost, CORAL is -1.77pp
   - CIs overlap for all three
   - Ordinal consistency (concordance) is similar: 0.821, 0.810, 0.825
   
2. **Binary (gen-1 vs gen-2+)**: CORAL edges out XGBoost by 0.62pp, though confidence intervals likely overlap.

The ordinal structure doesn't seem to provide meaningful gains with just 3 classes—CORAL actually underperforms XGBoost here, and Frank-Hall's marginal improvement isn't statistically significant. Checking this against my earlier binary results, the XGBoost performance aligns closely with what I found before, suggesting the experimental setup is consistent.

For the paper, I can directly address M5: ordinal-specific methods like CORAL and Frank-Hall don't meaningfully outperform standard multiclass XGBoost for 3-class depth estimation. Interestingly, CORAL does show a small advantage in the binary setting, hinting that ordinal structure might matter more when reducing the problem to pairwise comparisons.
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary synthetic text detection, providing a framework for characterizing the provenance of web-crawled text at finer granularity.
5	Across six generators from three model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency and diversity features drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features remain near-stationary.
6	A 7-feature ensemble combining both feature types achieves leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
7	We also reported that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling, establishing that, in our experimental setting, depth fingerprinting functions as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization rather than a curative tool for data selection.
8	
9	Future work should extend evaluation to instruction-tuned and RLHF models, frontier-scale generators (70B+), mixed-generator chains that better approximate real-world contamination, and multilingual settings.
10	All code, data, and trained models will be released upon publication to facilitate reproducibility.
11	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}
5	\label{sec:related-detection}
6	
7	Methods for distinguishing human-written from machine-generated text have evolved from statistical tests on token-level distributions \cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies} to zero-shot approaches exploiting reference-model likelihoods \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt} and learned classifiers over linguistic features \cite{feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}.
8	Recent work has pursued diverse detection signals: \citet{intrinsic_dimension} use intrinsic dimensionality estimates, \citet{diveye} propose surprisal diversity features directly comparable to our shape-based measures, and \citet{generator_barrier} characterize the distributional gap between human and model outputs.
9	\citet{wu_survey_2025} provide the most comprehensive recent survey; see also \citet{jawahar_survey} for an earlier overview.
10	A persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\ synthetic) decisions.
11	Recent efforts extend detection to multi-class settings: \citet{beyond_binary} propose role recognition and involvement measurement to distinguish collaborative human-LLM generation scenarios, and the SemEval-2024 shared task \cite{semeval2024_task8} evaluates multi-generator attribution across domains and languages.
12	These approaches discriminate among different \emph{sources} (human vs.\ specific models) rather than recursive generation \emph{depth} within a single generator's chain.
13	Even when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.
14	\citet{paraphrase_evades} and \citet{detection_possibilities} further demonstrate that detection robustness degrades under distributional shift.
15	Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.
16	
17	\paragraph{Source attribution vs.\ depth estimation.}
18	A related but distinct task is \emph{source attribution}: identifying \emph{which} model generated a given text.
19	\citet{uchendu_attribution} train multi-class classifiers over stylometric features to distinguish outputs of different generators, and \citet{munir_looking_glass} learn attribution models that generalize across unseen language models.
20	These methods answer \emph{who} generated the text; depth estimation answers \emph{how many recursive steps} produced it.
21	The two tasks are orthogonal: a single generator can produce text at any depth, and texts at the same depth may originate from different generators.
22	Our LOGO evaluation explicitly decouples generator identity from depth by holding out entire generators, ensuring that the classifier learns depth signal rather than generator fingerprints.
23	
24	\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}
25	\label{sec:related-collapse}
26	
27	A growing body of work studies quality degradation when models are trained on their own outputs.
28	\citet{self_consuming_mad} demonstrate that self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage, and \citet{curse_of_recursion} show that model collapse is inevitable in successive fine-tuning rounds without fresh human data, though \citet{model_collapse_avoidable} identify conditions under which careful data mixing can mitigate this effect.
29	\citet{strong_model_collapse} extend these results to larger-scale settings.
30	These studies focus on \emph{training-loop} degradation, where model weights are updated on recursive data.
31	Our setting is different: we study \emph{inference chains}, where a fixed model generates text conditioned on its own prior output without retraining.
32	
33	Two concurrent lines of work are most directly relevant.
34	\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, showing progressive information loss analogous to the telephone game.
35	\citet{markovian_chains} provide a theoretical framework for convergence in iterative text transformations, demonstrating that semantic-preservation constraints induce attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes.
36	Our work differs in two respects: we study \emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal beyond the attractor bound; and we focus on \emph{estimating} depth from text features rather than characterizing convergence behavior.
37	
38	\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}
39	\label{sec:related-diversity}
40	
41	Declining lexical diversity in LLM-generated text is documented in both training \cite{curious_decline} and deployment \cite{writing_diversity} settings.
42	Our surface features---type-token ratio and hapax ratio---instantiate this observation for inference chains, where we show monotonic diversity decline across generation depths without any model retraining.
43	
44	These features are classical stylometric measures with a long history in authorship attribution \cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}.
45	\citet{uchendu_attribution} extend stylometric and neural methods to multi-class attribution of text to specific generators.
46	We push beyond generator identity to the \emph{depth} dimension: text from the same generator at different depths exhibits systematically different feature profiles, and these profiles generalize across generators.
47	Sampling strategies also affect diversity; \citet{nucleus_sampling} show that top-$p$ sampling mitigates some degeneration, but our results demonstrate that diversity still declines monotonically across recursive application of the same sampling procedure.
48	
49	\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}
50	\label{sec:related-provenance}
51	
52	Scalable data curation relies on quality signals to filter web-crawled corpora.
53	\citet{dsir} select pretraining data to match a target distribution, \citet{qurating} learn quality scores from LLM judgments, and \citet{ccnet} filter Common Crawl via perplexity-based heuristics.
54	Generation depth is a complementary provenance signal: it characterizes \emph{how many steps removed} text is from a human source, orthogonal to content quality per se.
55	
56	An alternative approach is proactive provenance through watermarking \cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark}, where identifiable patterns are embedded during generation.
57	However, \citet{chainwash} demonstrate that watermark strength decays across multi-step generation chains, motivating passive post-hoc methods that do not require generator cooperation.
58	Our depth fingerprinting is such a method: it operates on text alone without assuming access to the generating model or an embedded watermark.
59	At the feature level, our reference-model NLL analysis connects to \citet{min_k_percent}, who use the probability distribution's lower tail for membership inference; we analyze the full NLL distribution for a different purpose---ordinal depth estimation rather than training-set membership.
60	
```

\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.\n\\citet{paraphrase_evades} and \\citet{detection_possibilities} further demonstrate that detection robustness degrades under distributional shift.\nOur reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Source attribution vs.\\ depth estimation.}\nA related but distinct task is \\emph{source attribution}: identifying \\emph{which} model generated a given text.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} train multi-class classifiers over stylometric features to distinguish outputs of different generators, and \\citet{munir_looking_glass} learn attribution models that generalize across unseen language models.\nThese methods answer \\emph{who} generated the text; depth estimation answers \\emph{how many recursive steps} produced it.\nThe two tasks are orthogonal: a single generator can produce text at any depth, and texts at the same depth may originate from different generators.\nOur LOGO evaluation explicitly decouples generator identity from depth by holding out entire generators, ensuring that the classifier learns depth signal rather than generator fingerprints.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}\n\\label{sec:related-collapse}\n\nA growing body of work studies quality degradation when models are trained on their own outputs.\n\\citet{self_consuming_mad} demonstrate that self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage, and \\citet{curse_of_recursion} show that model collapse is inevitable in successive fine-tuning rounds without fresh human data, though \\citet{model_collapse_avoidable} identify conditions under which careful data mixing can mitigate this effect.\n\\citet{strong_model_collapse} extend these results to larger-scale settings.\nThese studies focus on \\emph{training-loop} degradation, where model weights are updated on recursive data.\nOur setting is different: we study \\emph{inference chains}, where a fixed model generates text conditioned on its own prior output without retraining.\n\nTwo concurrent lines of work are most directly relevant.\n\\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, showing progressive information loss analogous to the telephone game.\n\\citet{markovian_chains} provide a theoretical framework for convergence in iterative text transformations, demonstrating that semantic-preservation constraints induce attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes.\nOur work differs in two respects: we study \\emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal beyond the attractor bound; and we focus on \\emph{estimating} depth from text features rather than characterizing convergence behavior.\n\n\\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}\n\\label{sec:related-diversity}\n\nDeclining lexical diversity in LLM-generated text is documented in both training \\cite{curious_decline} and deployment \\cite{writing_diversity} settings.\nOur surface features---type-token ratio and hapax ratio---instantiate this observation for inference chains, where we show monotonic diversity decline across generation depths without any model retraining.\n\nThese features are classical stylometric measures with a long history in authorship attribution \\cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} extend stylometric and neural methods to multi-class attribution of text to specific generators.\nWe push beyond generator identity to the \\emph{depth} dimension: text from the same generator at different depths exhibits systematically different feature profiles, and these profiles generalize across generators.\nSampling strategies also affect diversity; \\citet{nucleus_sampling} show that top-$p$ sampling mitigates some degeneration, but our results demonstrate that diversity still declines monotonically across recursive application of the same sampling procedure.\n\n\\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}\n\\label{sec:related-provenance}\n\nScalable data curation relies on quality signals to filter web-crawled corpora.\n\\citet{dsir} select pretraining data to match a target distribution, \\citet{qurating} learn quality scores from LLM judgments, and \\citet{ccnet} filter Common Crawl via perplexity-based heuristics.\nGeneration depth is a complementary provenance signal: it characterizes \\emph{how many steps removed} text is from a human source, orthogonal to content quality per se.\n\nAn alternative approach is proactive provenance through watermarking \\cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark}, where identifiable patterns are embedded during generation.\nHowever, \\citet{chainwash} demonstrate that watermark strength decays across multi-step generation chains, motivating passive post-hoc methods that do not require generator cooperation.\nOur depth fingerprinting is such a method: it operates on text alone without assuming access to the generating model or an embedded watermark.\nAt the feature level, our reference-model NLL analysis connects to \\citet{min_k_percent}, who use the probability distribution's lower tail for membership inference; we analyze the full NLL distribution for a different purpose---ordinal depth estimation rather than training-set membership.\n",
      "numLines": 60,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 60
    }
  },
